That leaves fragment attribution for Cellebrite/Magnet, and I could not find evidence that "which zlib fork produced this fragment" is a question those vendors actually need answered; they attribute artifacts via container metadata that is usually present. The thesis therefore survives as science but not yet as a funded product, and the founder's specific failure mode (specialised technique matched by a general baseline) recurs here in a different costume: matched not by a 3x bigger model but by a 2019 C++ heuristic that Microsoft ships in production.

**G1 — licence reality**

PASS, and this is the proposal's strongest gate — largely because the study does not need to redistribute a corpus at all.

VERIFIED: Govdocs1 / Digital Corpora (https://digitalcorpora.org/corpora/file-corpora/files/). Fetched. Terms are informal but explicit: files "are freely available for research and may be (to the best of our knowledge) freely redistributed." ~1,000,000 files originally, with "approximately 13,722 files have been removed from the original corpus of 1 million files" (~986k remain). Requested citation: Garfinkel, Farrell, Roussev and Dinolt, DFRWS 2009. Two real caveats: there is NO formal licence instrument (no CC/MIT/GPL), only a best-knowledge assertion, and the corpus knowingly retains live malware ("JavaScript exploits and Windows malware in mailing list archives") which the maintainers deliberately keep as "legitimately part of the corpus." Shipping a derived artifact from it means shipping malware-derived bytes — manageable, since only compressed derivatives of file content would be released, but it needs an explicit handling policy.

NOT VERIFIED: Software Heritage bulk-access terms. https://www.softwareheritage.org/legal/bulk-access-terms-of-use/ returned HTTP 503 on two separate attempts, and https://docs.softwareheritage.org/devel/getting-started/graph.html returned 404. I therefore cannot confirm the 27-billion-file claim's redistribution terms and am explicitly NOT asserting them. This is unverified, not verified-good.

WHY IT DOES NOT MATTER MUCH: the label manufacturer means the shippable artifact is a GENERATOR, not a dataset. Every encoder needed is already a distro package or stdlib (I confirmed on this box: libz.so.1.3, libdeflate.so.0, libzstd.so.1.5.5, libbrotlienc.so.1.1.0, gzip, xz, Java Deflater, Go compress/flate, Node zlib). Plaintext feedstock can be drawn from any DFSG-free Debian source or permissively-licensed code. So Phase 3 ships a script plus a seed manifest and reproduces 10^6 labelled streams in 4 minutes — no redistribution question ever arises. That is a cleaner licence posture than almost any corpus-based domain.

**G2 — label noise and degeneracy**

Two findings, one reassuring and one that invalidates the label schema as written.

NOT DEGENERATE (good, measured). I attacked the labels with trivial baselines and they held. Byte histograms of entropy-coded data are near-uniform by construction, so surface features carry almost nothing: size+ratio -> 9-way joint reached only 0.235 against 0.111 chance, and an unscaled logistic-regression baseline sat at chance across every feature set. There is no regex, no majority class, and no obvious nearest-neighbour surface feature that solves this. The label manufacturer is exact and free, and the label is genuinely absent from the artifact — raw DEFLATE carries no container header and zlib's FLEVEL is 2 advisory bits.

SYSTEMATICALLY WRONG LABELS (the real problem, measured). The proposed label set enumerates "zlib / zlib-ng / libdeflate / zopfli / 7-Zip / igzip / Java Deflater / Go flate / Apple / Cloudflare fork" as distinct classes. Several of those are not distinct functions — they are different language bindings over the same libz object code. I tested 40 real inputs x levels 1/6/9 and hashed the raw DEFLATE output:
  - Java Deflater vs CPython zlib: BYTE-IDENTICAL on 40/40 inputs at every level (120/120 total)
  - Node zlib vs CPython zlib: identical on 12%
  - Go compress/flate vs all three: 0% identical (genuinely independent reimplementation)
So "Java Deflater" and "zlib" are the same class. Training on this taxonomy injects irreducible label noise: identical X with different Y, capping accuracy below 100% for reasons that have nothing to do with model capacity and everything to do with a mislabelled ontology. Any scaling curve built on it would asymptote at a ceiling set by class collision and be misread as a capacity limit. The fix is mandatory: define labels by exercised code lineage plus parameter tuple, verified by hashing outputs and merging colliding classes, never by language binding or product name.

SECOND-ORDER RISK — NEAR-METADATA LEAKAGE. The first block's dynamic Huffman code-length table is effectively an encoder fingerprint sitting in a fixed, cheap-to-read location. From just 256 header bits plus a byte histogram, an off-the-shelf booster on 1,295 examples hit 0.805 implementation accuracy. That is not metadata in the container sense, but it is close enough that a study reporting header-visible numbers would be flattering itself. Honest evaluation must be fragment-based (I measured 0.605 there), or the "this is a real inverse problem" claim is doing less work than advertised.

THIRD RISK — DISTRIBUTION SHIFT. Manufactured streams come from whatever feedstock is chosen; real-world streams are dominated by a few content types and a few encoder/level combinations (zlib level 6 everywhere). A uniformly-sampled synthetic training set has a wildly different label prior than any deployment corpus, so headline accuracy will overstate field performance.

**G6 — CPU feasibility, concretely**

PASS with the largest margin of any gate. Every number below is measured on this actual box (4 cores, 15GB RAM, 29GB free on /, no GPU), not estimated.

UNIT SIZE. A fixed 4KB carved fragment is the natural unit; 2KB also works and halves everything. At 4,096 bytes/unit, 10^6 units = 4.1 GB on disk (measured arithmetic), comfortably inside the 29GB budget with room for four rungs plus checkpoints. Rungs 1k/10k/100k are 4MB/41MB/410MB — negligible.

DATA GENERATION. Measured in-process CPython zlib at 1,022 streams/s on ONE core with 40KB inputs. 10^6 labelled streams = 16.3 minutes single-core, 4.1 minutes across 4 cores. Generation is a rounding error, and it is regenerable rather than stored, so the disk figure can drop to near zero by generating on the fly. Note the measured contrast: my subprocess-based harness (spawning java/node per stream) managed only ~40 streams/s. The study must bind encoders in-process via ctypes against libz.so.1.3, libdeflate.so.0, libzstd.so.1.5.5 and libbrotlienc.so.1.1.0 (all confirmed present) rather than shelling out, a 25x difference that decides feasibility.

MODEL. Input is 4,096 bytes, or 32,768 bits if bit-unpacked. A 1D dilated CNN or small byte-level transformer over a 512-4096 length sequence at 1-5M parameters is the right size. At 5M params, fp32 weights plus Adam moments are ~60MB; with batch 64 and activations, peak RSS stays well under 2GB against 15GB available. No GPU is required and none of this is close to a memory constraint.

TRAINING COST. Anchored on measured cost: 4-fold CV of HistGradientBoosting over 1,295 x 514 features completed in ~2-3 minutes on 2 jobs. For the neural rungs, a 2-5M-param 1D CNN over 10^6 x 512-byte inputs runs roughly 3-6 CPU-hours per epoch on 4 cores. Budgeting 4-6 epochs at the top rung gives ~15-30 CPU-hours; the three lower rungs together cost under 10% of that (they are 0.1%, 1% and 10% of the data). Total for a full 4-rung curve spanning three orders of magnitude: well under 48 wall-clock hours on this box, and that is with slack for a couple of restarts.

The only genuine G6 risk is scope creep into brotli/LZMA/zstd simultaneously, which multiplies the label space without multiplying the compute budget. Restricted to DEFLATE, this fits easily.

**Strongest case against**

The brief's white-space claim is falsified by the primary source it cites, and the falsification lands precisely on the commercially valuable half of the problem.

microsoft/preflate-rs — production, Microsoft, updated 2026-07-25 — already performs encoder-implementation and parameter identification. Its README states the analysis stage "fingerprint[s] the original compressor: hash algorithm, chain depth, nice-length cutoff, window size, and block-splitting strategy," and tabulates detection across zlib (all levels), zlib-ng, libdeflate, miniz_oxide and Windows zlib at 0.01% reconstruction overhead on zlib at nine of ten levels. The brief asserts preflate "concedes" 20-30% failure and inferior detection to precomp; that language is absent from the current README and the published numbers contradict it for zlib. The single strongest piece of evidence offered for "heuristic identification is inadequate, learned identification is unexplored" does not survive contact with the document.

Stacked on top: with plaintext in hand, brute-force recompression is EXACT and took a measured 24 ms/stream over 36 candidates on this box. Every buyer named in G5 except forensics has the plaintext. So in the segment with budget — reproducible builds, CDN dedup, archival recompression — a deterministic method is exact, instant, already shipped, and cannot be beaten by a probabilistic classifier, only tied. In the segment where ML is genuinely the only option (carved fragments with no plaintext and no stream start, where I measured a real 0.605 vs 0.333), I found no evidence that any named buyer actually needs the answer.

Compounding this, the label ontology as written is wrong in a way that would silently corrupt the headline result: Java Deflater and CPython zlib emitted byte-identical raw DEFLATE on 40/40 inputs at every level I tested, because they are the same libz. Listing them as separate classes guarantees an accuracy ceiling that has nothing to do with scale.

And structurally, this is not the FDM-1 pattern. FDM-1's moat was 11M hours of screen recordings nobody else possessed. Here the oracle is zlib, which ships in every standard library on earth; I regenerated labelled data at 1,022 streams/s on a single core. Abundance is total and the moat is zero — any competitor, including an agentic model with a code sandbox, reconstructs the entire asset in minutes. The scarce input that made FDM-1 fundable has no analogue here.

### Adversarial review — CopybookLM: record-layout induction from undocumented fixed-width and EBCDIC byte streams

**Recommendation: REJECT.** G3 FAIL · G1 FAIL · G6 PASS. Reviewer confidence: High on G3 and G2 — those rest on my own measurements on this box (script at /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-raise-v1/5d675fa9-5fc4-501c-b3b3-a76510397d00/scratchpad/cb.py), and the flat 20-vs-20,000-record result and the 1.00 COMP-3 recall reproduce in seconds. High on the verified licence facts (the IPUMS redistribution ban and the CSV-only ACS PUMS directory were fetched directly). Medium on ICPSR, which I could not read through its Cloudflare challenge and have marked unverified rather than assumed. One caveat against myself: my generator uses independent random field content, the most pessimistic case for boundary identifiability; a generator fed genuinely correlated real tabular data would leave more learnable residue than I measured. That does not rescue the thesis — the residue is semantic and therefore favours the frontier model — but it does mean 0.78 is a lower bound on what a regex takes, not an upper one. Note also that web search was exhausted for this session before I began (200/200 used), so my crowding evidence comes from arXiv search pages, GitHub search, and direct source fetches rather than general web search.

**G3 — kill attempt**

I did not rely on speculation — I built the label manufacturer and a trivial non-learned baseline and measured it. Script: /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-raise-v1/5d675fa9-5fc4-501c-b3b3-a76510397d00/scratchpad/cb.py

Setup: random copybooks (5-25 fields; DISPLAY text, zoned PIC 9(n), COMP-3 packed with C/D/F sign nibble, COMP binary 2/4/8 byte), serialised to cp037 EBCDIC exactly as the domain proposes. Baseline: ~50 lines, zero learning — per-byte-offset value histogram, then a nibble/range rule per column (all bytes 0xF0-0xF9 = zoned; both nibbles 0-9 = packed body; low nibble in C/D/F = packed terminal; EBCDIC letter set = text; else binary), then greedy run segmentation.

Measured (exact field-boundary match, 30-40 random files per point):
  20 records/file:     F1 = 0.768 (P 0.846 / R 0.703)
  100 records/file:    F1 = 0.773
  1,000 records/file:  F1 = 0.752
  20,000 records/file: F1 = 0.782 (P 0.862 / R 0.715)
Type accuracy among matched fields = 1.000. COMP-3 field recall = 1.00 at every record count.

Three kills follow.

(1) THE STATED MOAT IS EMPIRICALLY FALSE. The G3 argument is "identifiable only by integrating evidence ACROSS 10^5 records, which no context window holds and no prompt expresses". Measured: 20 records -> 20,000 records moves F1 by +1.4 points. Cross-record aggregation saturates at a few dozen records. Fifty 200-byte records is 10KB of hex, which fits any 2025-2026 frontier context directly, and a per-column histogram featurizer is a 20-line script any coding agent writes unprompted. The non-text modality barrier is removed by a trivial wrapper. This is the founder's prior death scenario reproduced exactly: general model plus tiny script matches the specialised model.

(2) THE EASY MAJORITY IS A REGEX. Type tagging is 100% accurate from a deterministic nibble rule, and COMP-3 boundaries come free at recall 1.00 because the C/D/F terminal nibble is self-delimiting. The headline signal (packed-decimal nibble distributions, C/D/F sign nibbles, 0xF0-0xF9 digit runs) is a solved rule with no headroom and therefore no curve.

(3) THE HARD RESIDUE FORKS BOTH WAYS AGAINST THE THESIS. All remaining error is splitting runs of the SAME type (text recall 0.46-0.65, zoned 0.47-0.58): does this 8-digit run hold one field or a 5 and a 3. With random synthetic content that split is information-theoretically unidentifiable — Bayes error, flat curve, unfixable by data or parameters. With realistic content (the domain's own proposal, "serialise real tabular data through it") it becomes identifiable, but the signal is then a SEMANTIC prior (5+4 digits is ZIP+4, 6 digits is YYMMDD, 2 digits is an age) — precisely where frontier world knowledge dominates a small byte model and improves every generation. I also measured the non-learned entropy-jump detector (BinaryInferno's published method) on realistic-cardinality content: boundary recall 0.742 at 50 records rising only to 0.814 at 5,000, precision 0.76-0.82. Flat in data again.

Literature check: arXiv returned nothing for copybook/EBCDIC/fixed-width layout induction and no LLM binary-format-inference benchmark (https://arxiv.org/search/?searchtype=all&query=LLM+protocol+format+inference ; https://arxiv.org/search/?searchtype=all&query=EBCDIC+OR+copybook+OR+%22fixed-width%22+record+layout+inference). Absence of a benchmark cuts both ways; my own measurement supplies the missing evidence that the task is not hard in the way claimed. Note the cited "adjacent" BinaryInferno (NDSS 2023, P 0.69 / R 0.73) uses exactly the entropy method I reproduced — the technique transfers directly and only the corpus differs, so the 2/10 crowding score overstates the white space.

Commercial kill: modernisation vendors usually still hold the COBOL source, and the copybook lives in it. The "bytes lost AND source lost" intersection is far narrower than the patent quotes imply; and every public file in the proposed corpus ships a SAS/SPSS setup file, i.e. the answer.

**G3 — does it survive?**

It does not survive. The only genuinely defensible piece is the tail no heuristic touches: REDEFINES / OCCURS DEPENDING ON discriminator identification and record-length hypothesis search over an unblocked stream — combinatorial, not prompt-expressible, untouched by my baselines. But that rebuttal collapses on one fact: no public fixed-width corpus contains them. Census PUMS, CPS, NHANES, IRS SOI are plain ASCII fixed-width with no packed decimal, no binary COMP, no REDEFINES, no ODO. The only place the hard label exists is inside the researcher's own generator, so the curve would measure generator inversion, not a capability. That is not fundable and not defensible in review.

**G1 — licence reality**

Mixed, and materially worse than claimed.

PASS, verified public domain and redistributable: US Census fixed-width microdata. Census 2000 5-Percent PUMS is fixed-width ASCII and ships its layout as "5%_PUMS_record_layout.xls" in the same directory — https://www2.census.gov/census_2000/datasets/PUMS/FivePercent/ (verified by directory listing). US federal works carry no domestic copyright (https://www.usa.gov/government-copyright), so these are shippable. Same for CPS, NHANES, IRS SOI, CMS PUFs.

FAIL, verified NOT redistributable: IPUMS. Fetched https://www.ipums.org/about/terms — IPUMS International and IPUMS Full Count state verbatim "You will not redistribute the data." All other collections (USA, CPS, NHGIS, NHIS, MEPS, Time Use, etc.) allow only "publish a subset of the data to meet journal requirements for accessing data related to a particular publication. Contact us for permission for any other redistribution". IPUMS cannot be shipped in a Phase 3 artifact.

UNVERIFIED AND LIKELY A PROBLEM: ICPSR, the source of the claimed "tens of thousands of studies". https://www.icpsr.umich.edu/web/pages/ICPSR/access/index.html returns HTTP 403 behind a Cloudflare managed challenge (verified via curl with a browser UA); downloads require an authenticated account plus per-study agreements, deposits carry depositor-specific terms, and there is no bulk crawl path. Treat the 10^4 figure as unshippable until proven otherwise.

FACTUAL CORRECTION: modern ACS PUMS is no longer fixed-width. Directory listing of https://www2.census.gov/programs-surveys/acs/data/pums/2023/1-Year/ contains only csv_*.zip and sas_*.zip — no fixed-width file, no record layout. The fixed-width public corpus is historical (2000 decennial and earlier), not current.

THE REAL G1 DEFECT IS UNIT COUNTING. ">= 10^6 units" is met only by counting RECORDS, but every record in a file shares one label — the label-bearing unit is the FILE/LAYOUT, and within-file labels are perfectly correlated, so effective sample size equals the number of distinct layouts. Redistributable distinct layouts with published ground truth are order 10^2-10^3 (Census 2000 PUMS is ~2 record types across all 51 state files; CPS basic monthly is roughly one layout per year). Three to four orders short of 10^6. Only the synthetic half clears 10^6, and it needs no licence because it is not real.

**G2 — label noise and degeneracy**

Severe, and measured rather than asserted.

TRIVIAL-BASELINE DEGENERACY (measured): a ~50-line rule-based column classifier with zero learning reaches exact-boundary F1 0.78 with 100% type accuracy and COMP-3 recall exactly 1.00. Most of the label mass is a regex over nibble ranges. Any learned model's headline number starts at 0.78, and the "scaling curve" would be drawn over a ~0.22 residual.

BAYES-ERROR DEGENERACY: adjacent same-type fields serialised from random content carry zero information about the boundary. Measured: zoned-field recall pinned at 0.47-0.58 from 20 records to 20,000. A provably unrecoverable label is worse than a noisy one — it caps the curve, and the cap will be misread as a compute plateau, which is the worst possible outcome for a "data-constrained -> compute-constrained" thesis.

GENERATOR-PRIOR LEAKAGE: the model learns the generator's hyperparameters, not the world. My own uniform field-length priors (1-20 text, 1-9 zoned, 2/4/8 COMP) are an exploitable cue; a model can score well by memorising the length distribution and type mixture. Every synthetic-copybook study has this hole, and there is no held-out real set containing COMP-3 or REDEFINES to catch it.

SIM-TO-REAL GAP THE CALIBRATION SET CANNOT CLOSE: real sequential/VSAM data has RDW-prefixed variable-length records, block descriptors, mixed record types keyed by a discriminator, FILLER, sign overpunch (0xC0-0xC9 / 0xD0-0xD9 in the final zoned byte), implied decimal scaling, and correlated content. The proposed real half — plain ASCII fixed-width government microdata — has none of these, so the "measure the sim-to-real gap first" step can only validate the easy ASCII case.

LEAKAGE RISK: if splits are made by record rather than by layout, the test set is the training set. The G1 unit-counting error makes that mistake very easy to commit by accident.

**G6 — CPU feasibility, concretely**

G6 is the one gate this passes cleanly; I will state it straight rather than pile on.

Unit that works: one FILE as a per-byte-offset feature profile, not raw records. Record length L ~ 200 bytes typical (80-4000 range). A full 256-bin histogram per column is 256 x 200 x 4B = 205 KB/unit — too big (205 GB at 10^6, blows 30 GB disk). A compressed profile of ~32 summary features per column (nibble-range indicators, digit fraction, C/D/F terminal fraction, EBCDIC-letter fraction, entropy, distinct-value count, top-value frequency, byte mean/variance) is 200 x 32 x 2B fp16 = 12.8 KB/unit. At 10^6 units that is 12.8 GB on disk — fits in 30 GB alongside the 1k/10k/100k rungs and memory-maps comfortably inside 15 GB RAM.

Generation cost, measured on this box: my Python serialiser did 40 files x 5,000 records plus full histogramming in 7.5 s wall on one core = ~27k records/s. Since aggregation saturates by ~100 records (measured), 200 records per layout suffices. 10^6 layouts x 200 records = 2x10^8 serialisations = ~7,400 core-seconds ~ 2 core-hours in pure Python, under an hour on 4 cores; a numpy/C generator makes it minutes. Raw bytes never touch disk — profiles are computed streaming, only the 12.8 KB profile is written.

Model: 1D CNN or BiLSTM-CRF over 200 columns x 32 channels emitting per-column BIO boundary tags plus a 4-way type tag. 1-5M parameters, ~20 MB fp32. Forward+backward on a 200x32 sequence is ~10-50 MFLOPs; PyTorch CPU on 4 cores does order 10^10 FLOP/s, giving ~500-2,000 examples/s. One epoch over 10^6 units = 8-35 minutes; 10 epochs = 1.5-6 hours. Four rungs at 1k/10k/100k/1M with 10 epochs each total roughly 2-8 CPU-hours — under a day on 4 cores, no GPU.

So a real 4-rung curve over three orders of magnitude is comfortably achievable. G6 is not the reason to reject. The reason is that the curve would start at the 0.78 a regex already achieves and rise over a residual that is half unidentifiable and half a semantic-prior task a frontier model does better.

**Strongest case against**

The thesis rests on one empirical claim — that the label is identifiable only by aggregating across 10^5 records, so no prompt and no context window can express it — and I measured that claim to be false. A 50-line rule with zero learning reaches exact-boundary F1 0.768 from 20 records and 0.782 from 20,000; type tagging is 100% correct and COMP-3 recall is exactly 1.00 at every scale. Cross-record aggregation, the entire moat, is worth 1.4 F1 points. What remains is either information-theoretically unrecoverable (adjacent same-type fields over random content — a Bayes-error plateau that will masquerade as a compute plateau) or a natural-language semantic prior about what 5 digits followed by 4 digits means, which is exactly the regime where a bigger general model plus a 20-line histogram script wins and keeps winning. Compounding it: the redistributable real corpus supplies ~10^2-10^3 distinct layouts, not 10^6 (records share one label per file); IPUMS explicitly forbids redistribution; ICPSR is Cloudflare-gated and login-only; modern ACS PUMS is no longer fixed-width at all; and no public fixed-width file contains packed decimal, binary COMP, REDEFINES or OCCURS DEPENDING ON, so the only genuinely hard part of the label exists solely inside the researcher's own generator. That is a synthetic benchmark measuring generator inversion, wearing a real-corpus costume.

### Adversarial review — JVM bytecode build-provenance: which toolchain actually produced this .class file (compiler vendor + point version, build tool, flags), with a primary head of "declared provenance contradicts the bytecode"

**Recommendation: REJECT.** G3 FAIL · G1 FAIL · G6 PASS. Reviewer confidence: High. The decisive findings are not inferences from literature but direct measurements I ran on this box: five real javac binaries (11.0.32.1, 17.0.8, 17.0.13, 17.0.20.1, 21.0.10) compiling a real 215-file library, byte-compared class-by-class; and 12 real Maven Central jars (6,685 class files) whose manifests I read against their class-file headers. Both the zero-information result for point versions and the header-byte degeneracy result are reproducible in minutes. The G1 licence findings are quoted verbatim from robots.txt and the Terms of Service fetched today. The one thing I could not do is search the web for frontier-model benchmark numbers (the session's WebSearch budget was exhausted before my first query, DuckDuckGo returned a CAPTCHA, and Semantic Scholar returned 429 on three attempts) — but that evidence would only have strengthened an already-decided G3, since I killed the thesis on information-theoretic grounds rather than on competitive grounds. The only residual uncertainty is whether some reframed, coarser version (compiler family/generation and repackaging detection) could be salvaged; I judge it real but saturating far below 10^6 units and already occupied by APKiD, jNorm and DALEQ, which is why the recommendation is REJECT rather than BACKUP.

**G3 — kill attempt**

Two independent kills, one of which I established experimentally on this box rather than by argument.

KILL 1 — THE HEADLINE LABEL HAS ZERO MUTUAL INFORMATION WITH THE INPUT. I did not need a frontier model to beat this; I showed nothing can learn it. I downloaded three Adoptium JDK 17 point releases (17.0.8+7, 17.0.13+11, 17.0.20.1) plus JDK 11.0.32.1 and JDK 21.0.10, took a real library's real sources (commons-lang3 3.12.0 sources jar, 215 files -> 345 classes), and compiled it under each javac at fixed --release, then byte-compared every class file:
  --release 8 : javac 17.0.8 vs 17.0.13 -> 0 of 345 classes differ by a single byte (0.00%)
  --release 11: javac 17.0.8 vs 17.0.13 -> 0 of 335 classes differ (0.00%)
  --release 8 : javac 17.0.8 vs 17.0.20 -> 0 of 345 classes differ (0.00%)
  --release 11: javac 17.0.8 vs 17.0.20 -> 21 of 335 differ (6.27%)
The proposal's stated target is "a point version (javac 11.0.2 vs 11.0.9)... an empirical fingerprint whose resolution improves monotonically with the number of (artifact, toolchain) pairs". That is false as a matter of fact. For the adjacent point-release pair the channel capacity is exactly zero: identical bytes, 345/345. A scaling curve cannot go up over a zero-information channel. More data buys nothing. G3's "more compute makes it better" fails on its own terms, before any competitor model is considered. The 6.27% case shows point-release signal is sporadic and unpredictable (present for one adjacent pair, absent for another), which is worse than uniformly hard — you cannot even state a learning problem.

KILL 2 — THE MOAT ARGUMENT IS FACTUALLY WRONG FOR THE PART THAT IS LEARNABLE. The G3 defence rests on "the input is not human-legible... a bigger general model has no path to these quirks because they are not described anywhere in text". Both halves fail. (a) `javap -v` ships in every JDK and renders a .class file as plain English text: the literal line "major version: 61", a printed constant-pool listing, and a named attribute list. Piping `javap -v Foo.class | head -40` into any frontier model is plain prompting, is trivially expressible, and the disassembly format is saturated in pretraining (StackOverflow, the JVM spec, every bytecode tutorial). (b) The quirks that DO carry signal are among the most heavily documented changes in the language: JEP 280 (string concat moved to invokedynamic at 9), JEP 181 nestmates removing synthetic access$ bridges at 11, the class-file version table, `-g`/`-parameters` attribute semantics. These are exactly the things a frontier model has read. My own measurement confirms how shallow the learnable residue is: javac 21 vs javac 17 at the same --release is detectable only because javac 21 emits a MethodParameters attribute on synthetic constructors — `b'MethodParameters' in classbytes` gives 100% precision, and on 65.8% of classes the two compilers' output is byte-identical so nothing can separate them.

KILL 3 — THE FIELD'S OWN EVIDENCE POINTS THE WRONG WAY. Dietrich et al., "Levels of Binary Equivalence" (arXiv 2410.08427), built 14,156 pairs of jars independently built by Google, Oracle and RedHat and found only 3,750 pairs (26.49%) contain even one differing class file. 73.51% of jar pairs built in genuinely different environments by different vendors are bitwise identical. The proposal cites this paper as adjacent prior art without noticing it is the strongest published measurement AGAINST the thesis: build environment usually leaves no trace in the bytecode at all.

I could not run web searches for frontier-model benchmark numbers (this session's 200-call WebSearch budget was exhausted before my first query; DuckDuckGo HTML returned a CAPTCHA and Semantic Scholar returned HTTP 429 on three attempts). I compensated by running the discriminative experiment directly, which is stronger evidence than a benchmark number would have been.

**G3 — does it survive?**

It does not survive as proposed. The primary label is provably not a function of the input.

The most honest rescue available, stated fairly: drop point versions entirely and retarget to compiler FAMILY and GENERATION plus repackaging detection (javac-8-era vs javac-11-era vs javac-17+-era vs ecj vs kotlinc vs "passed through ProGuard/R8/shade/a decompile-recompile cycle"). That signal is real and deep — I measured javac 11.0.32 vs javac 17.0.20 at fixed --release as 345/345 classes byte-differing, with the difference being a global reordering of the constant pool (this_class moves from #10 to #8; 2,064 diff lines in javap output), not a single attribute. That is a genuine distributed structural fingerprint of the kind the proposal claims.

But this rescue kills the funding thesis for a different reason: the resulting label space is roughly 10-20 coarse classes, it is exactly what APKiD already does with a few dozen hand-written YARA rules, and what jNorm/DALEQ (arXiv 2508.01530) already enumerate as datalog normalisation rules. A 10-20 class problem with a distributed but stationary fingerprint saturates at a few thousand examples. It does not need 10^6 units and its accuracy curve flattens long before the top rung, so it fails "the curve goes up with compute" a second time, now by saturation rather than by zero information. There is no version of this domain where 10^6 units is the binding constraint.

**G1 — licence reality**

FAIL on both access and redistribution. The claim "Maven Central, publicly and bulk-downloadable" is not true as of 2026.

1. robots.txt explicitly forbids it AND reserves rights against AI training. Fetched today, https://repo1.maven.org/robots.txt and https://repo.maven.apache.org/robots.txt are identical and contain:
   `User-agent: *` / `Content-Signal: search=yes,ai-train=no,use=reference` / then `User-agent: *` / `Disallow: /`, with only `User-agent: Googlebot` `Allow: /`.
   The file states verbatim: "ANY RESTRICTIONS EXPRESSED VIA CONTENT SIGNALS ARE EXPRESS RESERVATIONS OF RIGHTS UNDER ARTICLE 4 OF THE EUROPEAN UNION DIRECTIVE 2019/790". So the corpus host has (a) disallowed all non-Google crawlers, and (b) made an express Article 4 DSM opt-out from text-and-data-mining for AI training. This is the single most direct possible legal signal against the proposed use.

2. The Terms of Service name bulk download as abuse. https://central.sonatype.org/terms.html (Central Repository Terms of Service, "Last updated on March 31, 2026") lists as "Abusive Actions": "Attempting to download all or large portions of the Materials stored in Central (a.k.a., scraping or mirroring any content)"; "Excessive consumption of Materials, artifacts or metadata"; "Attempts to defeat or evade abuse detection mechanisms". The same document announces a tiered "Consumption Tier" model with Paid Consumption Tiers for high-volume consumers. https://central.sonatype.org/faq/429-error/ documents escalating blocks up to 24 hours, and states "attempts to evade limits, or clear abuse may result in longer or permanent blocks". Acquiring 10^6 class files means pulling on the order of 5,000-15,000 jars; that is squarely the described pattern, and the "I provide tooling that fetches Java components" and "I am an infrastructure provider" FAQ pages both route such use to a commercial conversation with Sonatype.

3. The Google mirror is not a loophole. https://maven-central.storage-download.googleapis.com/maven2/... does serve (I verified HTTP 200 and used it for this diligence, at a dozen-jar scale). Using it at 10^4-jar scale to route around Central's rate limiting is precisely "attempts to defeat or evade abuse detection mechanisms" under the ToS above, and it is Google's infrastructure provisioned for Google's own consumers, not a sanctioned research mirror.

4. Redistribution — the Phase 3 blocker — is worse than the access problem. The ToS is explicit that Sonatype grants nothing over the artifacts: "Third party Materials available on Central may be accompanied by their own licenses or terms of use. The applicable licenses or terms of use which accompany any Materials you download will govern your rights". A .class file is the compiled copyrighted work itself. Maven Central is a heterogeneous mix of Apache-2.0, GPL, LGPL, EPL, CDDL and outright proprietary-but-freely-downloadable artifacts. There is no blanket right to redistribute a corpus of 10^6 class files, and clearing licences per-artifact at that scale is not feasible. Shipping only derived structural token sequences is more defensible but is a derivative work of an expressly ai-train=no corpus, and it also means the open artifact cannot be reconstructed without a downloader that itself violates the anti-mirroring clause.

The proposal's fallback ("recompile source-available projects under a matrix of JDKs in Docker") is fully legal and is in fact exactly what I did today — but it is synthetic generation, not a pre-existing unlabelled corpus, so it dissolves FDM-1 pattern part 1 (abundant data that already exists) and makes the whole abundance argument moot.

**G2 — label noise and degeneracy**

The label manufacturer is broken in four separate ways. I verified all four against 12 real, popular Maven Central jars (commons-lang3 3.12.0 and 3.17.0, guava 32.1.3, jackson-databind 2.15.2, slf4j-api 2.0.9, okhttp 4.12.0, kotlin-stdlib 1.9.22, netty-common 4.1.100, junit-jupiter-api 5.10.1, jetty-util 11.0.18, commons-io 2.15.1, httpclient 4.5.14 — 6,685 class files total).

(1) SYSTEMATICALLY WRONG BY CONSTRUCTION: `javac --release N` is specified to produce output independent of the running JDK, so the declared Build-Jdk is not a function of the bytecode. Observed directly: commons-io 2.15.1 declares `Build-Jdk-Spec: 21` while all 338 main classes are class-file major 52 (Java 8). guava 32.1.3 declares `Build-Jdk-Spec: 11`, all 2,020 classes major 52. slf4j-api 2.0.9 declares `Build-Jdk-Spec: 20`, 51 of 52 classes major 52. commons-lang3 3.17.0 declares 17, classes are major 52. The label and the input are decoupled by design, which is a Bayes-error floor, not a hard problem.

(2) CAUSALLY UNRELATED LABEL: 7 of the 12 jars record `Created-By: Apache Maven Bundle Plugin 5.1.8/5.1.9`. That is the OSGi manifest-writing plugin (bnd). It compiled nothing. Training a model to predict "Apache Maven Bundle Plugin 5.1.9" from bytecode is training it to predict a string produced by a later, unrelated pipeline stage.

(3) MISSING NOT AT RANDOM: 2 of 12 jars — okhttp 4.12.0 and kotlin-stdlib 1.9.22 — have literally zero provenance keys in MANIFEST.MF. These are the Gradle-built, Kotlin-ecosystem artifacts. Gradle's jar task does not write Created-By/Build-Jdk. So the "answer key" exists for Maven-built jars and vanishes for Gradle-built ones, which is a large and non-random slice of the ecosystem including all of Android and Kotlin. Worse, the PRIMARY head ("declared provenance is inconsistent with the bytecode") is undefined precisely where nothing is declared — i.e. undefined on the population you most want to police. Also note the multi-release jars (commons-io, commons-lang3 3.17, jackson, slf4j, kotlin-stdlib) each mix major 52 and 53 class files under one jar-level manifest, so the per-class label inherited from that manifest is wrong for the META-INF/versions/ classes.

(4) TRIVIALLY PREDICTABLE RESIDUE — a regex beats the model on what remains. Controlled test, javac 21.0.10, same source, `--release 8/11/17/21`: the release-11, release-17 and release-21 outputs are byte-for-byte identical except the 2-byte major-version field at offset 6-7 (I diffed bytes[8:] across all pairs: identical, 1,097 bytes each). So the entire recoverable target is `struct.unpack('>H', b[6:8])`. `-g` level is attribute-presence (`LineNumberTable`/`LocalVariableTable` in the bytes; sizes 1105 vs 818). `-parameters` is `MethodParameters` in the bytes. kotlinc is the `kotlin/Metadata` annotation. javac 21 vs javac 17 at fixed --release reduces to one substring test with 100% precision and 17.6% recall (120/680 classes), while 65.8% of classes are byte-identical between the two. A 20-line script with five boolean features captures essentially all of the signal, and the part it misses is the part I showed is unlearnable.

Net: the manufactured label is part deterministic-header-byte (trivial), part causally-unrelated-plugin-string (noise), part structurally-absent (selection bias), and part provably-not-in-the-input (irreducible). That is a textbook degenerate label manufacturer.

**G6 — CPU feasibility, concretely**

G6 is the one gate this domain genuinely clears, measured rather than guessed.

UNIT SIZE: over the 6,685 real class files in my 12-jar sample, mean uncompressed .class size is 3,707 bytes, median 2,014 bytes, p90 7,316 bytes. Total 24.8 MB uncompressed from 11.2 MB of jars (2.2x compression).

CORPUS AT 10^6 UNITS: ~3.7 GB of raw class bytes, or ~1.7 GB held as jars and decompressed on the fly. Reduced to the proposed structural token stream (constant-pool tag sequence + opcode stream + attribute-name tags) truncated to 512 tokens, that is 10^6 x 512 x 2 bytes = ~1.0 GB as uint16, or ~2.0 GB at seq 1024. Comfortably inside the 30 GB writable disk (this box currently shows 27 GB free on /).

MODEL: a classification head, not generative modelling, so a 5-20M parameter transformer (d_model 256, 6 layers, seq 256-512) is ample; at fp32 that is 20-80 MB of weights plus optimiser state, i.e. under 500 MB, leaving the 15 GB RAM almost entirely for the data shard. A byte/opcode-n-gram gradient-boosted tree baseline fits in well under 4 GB.

CPU-HOURS FOR A 4-RUNG CURVE (1k/10k/100k/1M) on 4 cores, no GPU: rungs 1k and 10k are minutes; 100k is a few hours; the 1M rung at seq 256 with a ~10M-param model runs roughly 2-5k tokens/s on 4 cores, so 2.6x10^8 tokens/epoch lands at ~15-35 CPU-hours per epoch, i.e. ~1-2 days wall clock for a small number of epochs. Total for all four rungs plus the trivial baselines: on the order of 2-4 days. That is inside the brief.

CAVEAT, and it belongs to G1 not G6: torch is not installed here (verified), which is minor, but acquiring the 10^6 units requires pulling roughly 5,000-15,000 jars from a repository whose robots.txt disallows all non-Google agents and whose ToS names exactly that as an Abusive Action with escalating blocks. The box can train it; you cannot lawfully fill the box.

**Strongest case against**

The primary label is provably not a function of the input, and I measured it: compiling a real 345-class library with javac 17.0.8 and javac 17.0.13 at `--release 8` produced 345 out of 345 byte-identical class files, and at `--release 11`, 335 out of 335 byte-identical. Zero bits. The pitch's central claim — that point-version discrimination is "an empirical fingerprint whose resolution improves monotonically with the number of pairs" — is false; no quantity of data raises a curve over a channel of zero capacity. Meanwhile the residue that IS recoverable collapses to a two-byte header field (the release-11/17/21 outputs of javac 21 are byte-identical apart from bytes 6-7) plus a handful of attribute-presence substring tests, which a 20-line script does exactly as well as a neural model, and which a frontier model also does under plain prompting because `javap -v` renders the whole input as human-legible English documented all over the pretraining corpus. On top of that the corpus is legally closed — repo1.maven.org's robots.txt is `Disallow: /` for everything but Googlebot and carries an express `ai-train=no` Article 4 EU DSM reservation, while the March 2026 Central ToS names bulk download and mirroring as an Abusive Action. This is a domain where the hard part is impossible, the possible part is trivial, and the data cannot be lawfully obtained or redistributed.

### Adversarial review — Serial-crystallography indexing from sparse Bragg peak lists (peak list -> unit cell + orientation matrix A, SFX/SSX failure tail)

**Recommendation: BACKUP.** G3 MARGINAL · G1 PASS · G6 PASS. Reviewer confidence: Medium-high on G1, G2 and G6 - those rest on direct measurement of the actual corpus (246 CXIDB pages scraped, 46 stream files enumerated with sizes, 4 MB of a real CrystFEL stream range-downloaded and 444 chunks parsed, licence text pulled from cxidb.org/deposit.html, robots.txt read, COD licence and 534,680 count confirmed, Kirkwood 2022 Data Records read via Europe PMC PMC9005607). Medium on G3: my WebSearch budget was exhausted before I could run any frontier-model benchmark searches, so the LLM evidence comes only from arXiv API metadata (6 hits for crystallography+LLM, 15 for diffraction+LLM, all QA benchmarks or tool-orchestrating agents, none doing reciprocal-space geometry) plus the fetched abstract of arXiv:2511.22875. I did not verify the FINDEXER "~50% of XFEL microcrystal samples fail cell-finding" figure directly, though my own measurement of 145/444 = 32.7% non-indexed on CXIDB 152 is consistent in order of magnitude. G4 crowding I only partially re-checked: arXiv all:"serial crystallography" AND all:"neural" returns exactly ONE paper, PeakNet (2023-03-24), which is peak FINDING (image -> peaks), not indexing (peaks -> A). The specific slot "learned indexer for sparse SFX peak lists" does appear genuinely empty in the arXiv record - that part of the white-space claim holds up.

Recommendation rationale: BACKUP rather than REJECT because G1 and G6 are unusually solid (verified CC0, verified >10^6 already-computed labelled pairs, verified sub-GB working set, ~21 CPU-hours for a 4-rung curve) and the anti-LLM moat is real and permanent. BACKUP rather than SELECT because the label manufacturer cannot label the failure tail that is the entire commercial premise, the measured trivial baselines are strong, and four zero-data classical competitors in 24 months reproduce the exact failure mode that killed the founder's last company. If pursued, the framing must change: not "predict indexability" (degenerate) and not "distil CrystFEL" (ceiling-bound), but "learned outlier and multi-lattice decomposition trained on COD-forward-simulated pathologies, validated on real CXIDB streams by merging statistics rather than per-pattern labels."

**G3 — kill attempt**

Two attacks; the second is the dangerous one.

(1) THE LLM ATTACK FAILS. I could not find any evidence a frontier general model does this or a close proxy. WebSearch budget was exhausted, so I queried the arXiv API directly. Searching all:"crystallography" AND all:"large language model" returns 6 papers total, and all:"diffraction" AND all:"large language model" returns 15. Every single one is either (a) a question-answering benchmark - OPENXRD (2025-07-12), MatQnA (2025-09-14), MaterialFigBENCH (2026-03-12), MatVQA (2025-05-23) - or (b) an agent that ORCHESTRATES classical software rather than doing the numerics: "Rongzai agent: A Large Language Model-Based Autonomous Assistant for Rietveld Refinement of Neutron Diffraction Data" (2026-05-13), "NeuDiff Agent: A Governed AI Workflow for Single-Crystal Neutron Crystallography" (2026-02-18), "Harnessing AtomisticSkills for Agentic Atomistic Research" (2026-05-18). Nobody prompts an LLM to regress astar/bstar/cstar from 24 (fs, ss, 1/d, I) tuples, because it is not a coherent operation. I verified the input format on real data: a chunk is 24 rows of "96.50 1828.50 2.11 1732.53 p3a4". Scaling a text model does not touch this. The letter of G3 is satisfied.

(2) THE REAL ATTACK: THE CHEAP UNTRAINED METHOD, WHICH IS EXACTLY HOW THE FOUNDER'S LAST COMPANY DIED. The competitor is not a bigger LLM, it is a few hundred lines of hand-designed optimisation with zero training data, and there have been FOUR in 24 months all aimed at precisely this failure tail: TORO (J. Appl. Cryst. 2024), FINDEXER (Struct. Dyn., Oct 2025), MCDPS-SFX (Appl. Sci. 2026), and most damagingly Nasser, Poitevin & Dalton, "Robust Indexing for Challenging Serial X-ray Diffraction Patterns" (arXiv:2511.22875, submitted 28 Nov 2025, SLAC) - which I fetched and confirmed is a CLASSICAL symmetry-aware lattice-decoding algorithm with a dedicated small-N objective mode for "patterns with few reliable peaks, background contamination, or skewed unit cells," evaluated on three XFEL protein datasets, reporting it "matches or exceeds established indexers like XGANDALF and TORO." That is the target the learned indexer must beat, it appeared while the data would still be being collected, it needs no corpus, no GPU, no labels, and it ships as a library. This is the same structural death: a general, cheap, training-free method catches up to the specialised data-heavy one.

(3) SCALE MONOTONICITY IS THIN ON MECHANISM. Ask what actually accumulates with more patterns. Orientation is uniformly random BY CONSTRUCTION (randomly oriented microcrystals in a jet) - there is no population prior over orientation to learn, ever. The unit-cell vocabulary saturates almost immediately: the entire CXIDB stream corpus is ~20 experiments covering on the order of 40 distinct cells, and within a single run the cell is constant (I measured a = 11.84 nm +/- 0.02 across 299 consecutive indexed chunks in CXIDB 152). Detector-pathology priors are facility- and geometry-specific and do not transfer across the five detector types in the archive. The only genuinely data-hungry component is a learned outlier/multi-lattice model (rejecting ice rings, jet debris, second lattices, hot pixels). That is real but unproven, and there is a live risk the curve flattens by 10^4-10^5 units, which is the "curve stops going up" failure.

**G3 — does it survive?**

PARTIALLY, and only on the modality argument.

It survives the LLM test outright and I am confident about that: the arXiv record shows the frontier-model path into crystallography is agentic tool-calling, not numerical geometry, and an agent that calls XGANDALF cannot solve patterns XGANDALF fails on - retrying with different parameters is what facilities already do. The moat against language models is real and permanent.

It does NOT cleanly survive attack (2). The best rebuttal is that the learned model amortises across a run while every classical indexer restarts a combinatorial search per pattern, so a learned prior over "which peaks belong to the target lattice" is information the classical methods structurally cannot use - and Nasser et al. themselves needed a hand-crafted "small-N objective mode," i.e. they are hand-coding by inspection the prior a model could learn from data. That is a genuine argument. But it is an argument, not evidence, and the burden is on the learned method to beat a baseline that has improved four times in two years at zero data cost.

Attack (3) I consider only weakly rebutted. The honest version of the scale story is not "indexing yield rises with patterns seen" - it is "outlier-rejection quality rises with pathology diversity seen," and the corpus has roughly 20 experiments' worth of pathology diversity, not 10^6.

**G1 — licence reality**

PASS on licence, but the proposal names the WRONG corpus and the correction matters.

LICENCE, VERIFIED: CXIDB's depositor terms state "All deposited data and metadata are made available under the CC0 waiver to promote maximum reuse" (https://www.cxidb.org/deposit.html). Every individual deposit page I scraped carries the line "Licensed under the CC0 Public Domain Dedication Waiver. Please give proper credit via citations." CC0 is a rights waiver, so redistribution of a derived (peak list -> A matrix) corpus is unambiguously permitted - Phase 3 shipping is clean. https://www.cxidb.org/robots.txt contains only Cloudflare's content-signals explanatory preamble with NO Disallow lines and NO content-signal values set, so under its own clause (c) nothing is granted or restricted; the CC0 dedication on the data files governs regardless. Crystallography Open Database: "All data in the COD and the database itself are dedicated to the public domain and licensed under the CC0 License," 534,680 entries as of 2026-08-19 (https://www.crystallography.net/cod/).

THE CORPUS CORRECTION: the proposal claims CXIDB ID 185 (~19M images) gives peak arrays already stored inside CXI files so "the working corpus is sub-GB." That is FALSE. I pulled the Data Records section of Kirkwood et al., Sci. Data 2022 via Europe PMC full text (PMC9005607, doi 10.1038/s41597-022-01266-w): "The data deposited in the Coherent X-ray imaging Data Bank (CXIDB) contains approximately 19 million images in HDF5 format... Raw data are located in the raw directory... RAW-R0083-AGIPD00-S00000.h5... The calibrated data are then stored in CORR-R0083-AGIPD00-S00000.h5." Tables 6 and 7 list only raw/calibrated AGIPD intensity, gain and mask arrays. No peak lists, no CrystFEL streams, no indexing solutions. ID 185 is multi-TB of raw detector frames and is NOT usable as a label source on a 30GB disk.

THE CORPUS THAT ACTUALLY WORKS, VERIFIED BY MEASUREMENT: I scraped all 246 CXIDB deposit pages and found 46 CrystFEL .stream files, totalling 112.6 GB as listed (e.g. CXIDB 180 large-BW-pinkIndexer.stream 31.46 GB; CXIDB 161 five streams of 1.9-10.1 GB each bz2; CXIDB 100 four streams 0.16-3.48 GB; CXIDB 62 all-amb.stream.bz2 6.09 GB; CXIDB 45 ProK-native.stream.bz2 2.84 GB), across ~20 deposits, all CC0, all served over plain HTTP with byte-range support (I range-fetched the first 4 MB of https://www.cxidb.org/data/152/KDO8PS.stream directly). Measured 445 chunks per 4 MB = 8.9 KB mean per chunk, so 112.6 GB listed (of which ~63 GB is bz2, ~5x expansion) is on the order of 10-40 million chunks, ~2/3 of them carrying an indexed crystal block with astar/bstar/cstar. That is >10^6 and plausibly >10^7 exact labelled pairs. G1 PASSES - for a different reason than the proposal gives.

**G2 — label noise and degeneracy**

HIGH, and one of the failure modes is structural rather than fixable with more data. I measured all of this on the real CXIDB 152 stream head (444 complete chunks).

(a) THE CELL LABEL IS ASSERTED, NOT DISCOVERED, AND IS CONSTANT. The stream header shows the depositor ran `indexamajig ... --indexing=xgandalf,xds,mosflm,dirax,asdf -p cubic.cell`. A target cell file was SUPPLIED. Across all 299 indexed chunks the refined cell is a = 11.84 nm +/- 0.02 with all angles 90 +/- 0.2 deg - constant to 0.2%. A constant predictor recovers the unit cell essentially perfectly within any run, and across the whole archive the cell is a ~40-way classification with heavy imbalance. Worse, the peak rows already contain a precomputed (1/d) column, so a histogram of |q| is a direct powder-ring fingerprint of the cell. Cell prediction is regex-tier.

(b) MEASURED TRIVIAL BASELINE ON THE HEADLINE METRIC. If the study frames itself as "predict indexability / raise indexing yield," I tested it: 299/444 chunks indexed = 67.3% majority-class accuracy; a SINGLE THRESHOLD on num_peaks (t >= 14) gets 81.3%. Indexed chunks have mean 27.4 peaks (median 23), non-indexed have mean 18.4 (median 12). One integer captures most of the signal. That framing is degenerate.

(c) THE FATAL ONE - SELECTION BIAS THAT BREAKS THE VPT ANALOGY. Labels exist only where a classical indexer succeeded. The thesis targets the 33-50% where it failed, and on that population there is ZERO A-matrix ground truth, by construction, forever. In VPT the label manufacturer was strictly STRONGER than the learner: the IDM saw future frames the policy never gets, so distilling it transferred real information. Here CrystFEL sees exactly the same peak list the student sees - no information asymmetry at all. A student trained to imitate CrystFEL on CrystFEL's successes converges to CrystFEL, at best. This is not a noise problem you fix with more patterns; it is the wrong shape of manufacturer.

(d) EVALUATION AMBIGUITY. The A matrix is defined only modulo the lattice point group (48 equivalent orientations for m-3m) and modulo indexing ambiguity - CXIDB 203's own README instructs users to run `ambigator -y m-3 -w m-3m`, and CXIDB 152's stream was itself re-indexed by ambigator. Naive matrix-MSE loss or naive angular error will be systematically wrong unless quotiented, and getting that wrong silently inflates or destroys the reported numbers.

WHAT PARTIALLY REDEEMS IT: the non-indexed chunks ARE deposited with their full peak lists (145 of 444 here), so the failure population is available as input even without labels; and merging statistics (CC1/2, CC*, Rsplit against the deposited MTZ/PDB) are a genuine free environment-supplied validator - indexing garbage degrades them - so a real-data claim can be checked without per-pattern labels. The forward-simulation arm from COD does produce labels on the hard tail, but the moment you lean on it you are training on synthetics, which is exactly LaueNN and AIdex territory, and the sim-to-real gap is precisely the pathologies nobody can simulate well.

**G6 — CPU feasibility, concretely**

Comfortably feasible; this is the strongest gate. All numbers derived from measurement, not estimate.

UNIT SIZE. Measured on the real stream: mean 8.9 KB of TEXT per chunk (min 1.05 KB, max 64 KB), 24.4 peaks mean (min 10, max 246), 126.7 predicted reflections per indexed chunk. Parsed to a compact binary record: peaks as (fs, ss, 1/d, I) x float32 = 16 B/peak. At the measured mean of 24 peaks that is 384 B; capping at 200 peaks worst-case gives 3.2 KB. Label = 9 float32 A matrix (astar/bstar/cstar) = 36 B, plus lattice_type, centering, indexed_by flag and a run/geometry id = ~32 B. Call it ~450 B mean, ~1 KB with padding overhead.

CORPUS AT 10^6 UNITS. ~0.45 GB packed, ~1-3 GB if stored padded to a fixed 200-peak tensor. Against 30 GB writable disk that leaves ample room for all four rungs, a simulated arm, and shards. Ingest cost: 10^6 chunks x 8.9 KB = ~9 GB of stream text, i.e. two or three of the mid-size deposits, or piped as `curl | bunzip2 | parser` so nothing large ever lands on disk. Hours of network, not days. (Note: harvesting the FULL 112.6 GB would be a multi-day network job and one single file, CXIDB 180's 31.46 GB uncompressed stream, exceeds the disk - so streaming-and-reducing is mandatory, not optional.)

MODEL. Point-set / set-transformer over the peak set, d_model = 128, 4 layers, 4 heads: ~1.5M parameters. Params + Adam moments = ~25 MB. Activations at batch 256 with N padded to 64 peaks: ~100 MB. Total well under 1 GB against 15 GB RAM - the box is over-provisioned by an order of magnitude.

CPU-HOURS FOR A 4-RUNG CURVE ON 4 CORES. Per-sample forward FLOPs at N = 64: 4 layers x (4·N·d^2 projections + 2·N^2·d attention) = 4 x (4·64·16384 + 2·4096·128) ~= 4 x (4.2M + 1.05M) ~= 21 MFLOP; forward+backward ~= 63 MFLOP. Four cores with AVX2 fp32 through PyTorch/MKL realistically deliver 8-20 GFLOP/s sustained; taking a conservative 10 GFLOP/s gives ~160 samples/s, and small-N attention is latency- not FLOP-bound so 150-400/s is the realistic band.
Rung schedule: 1k x 200 epochs = 2x10^5 steps; 10k x 100 = 1x10^6; 100k x 40 = 4x10^6; 1M x 10 = 1x10^7. Total ~= 1.5x10^7 sample-steps. At 200 samples/s that is ~21 hours wall-clock for one full curve. With three seeds at the small rungs, the COD-simulated arm, and evaluation, budget 2-3 days. That is inside "within days."

No GPU is needed anywhere: the label manufacturer is already-computed CrystFEL output (pure parsing, no re-indexing), the simulation arm is analytic reciprocal-space geometry, and the model is small enough that CPU attention over 24-200 points is cheap. G6 is not the constraint here.

**Strongest case against**

The label manufacturer is definitionally blind to the population the thesis is about, and it is not stronger than the learner - which is the one property that made VPT work.

Every free label comes from a classical indexer succeeding. The product claim is "index the 33-50% that classical indexers discard." There is therefore no ground truth, and no way to manufacture ground truth from the real archive, on the exact patterns the buyer cares about. Unlike VPT - where the IDM saw future frames and so genuinely knew something the student's input could not contain - CrystFEL here sees the identical peak list the student sees. Distilling it has an information ceiling equal to CrystFEL itself. The escape hatch is forward simulation from COD, but that converts the study into synthetic-trained lattice recovery, which is what LaueNN and AIdex already are, and moves the entire risk into a sim-to-real gap over precisely the pathologies (multi-lattice, ice, jet debris, partiality, wrong-cell contaminants) that nobody knows how to simulate faithfully - because if they did, the patterns would already index.

Compounding it: I measured that the obvious headline metric is near-degenerate (81.3% indexability accuracy from a single num_peaks threshold vs 67.3% majority), the cell half of the label is supplied by the depositor via `-p cubic.cell` and is constant to 0.2% within a run, the orientation half is uniformly random by construction so there is no population prior to accumulate, and the cell vocabulary across the whole archive is ~40 - so it is genuinely unclear what keeps improving from 10^5 to 10^6 units. Meanwhile four training-free classical indexers have attacked this same failure tail since 2024, the newest (arXiv:2511.22875, Nov 2025, SLAC) reporting it matches or exceeds XGANDALF and TORO with zero data. That is the founder's previous death replayed with a different general method.

## Round 1: known limitations of this round

- Two adversary agents reported their WebSearch quota exhausted (200/200) before issuing queries and fell back to direct arXiv/Europe PMC/HTTP fetches. Their G3 external-evidence coverage is therefore weaker than their on-box measurements. Recorded rather than smoothed over.
- Two screened survivors (Headerless load-layout recovery, ProcedureNet) were cut by the top-4 finalist slice and were NOT adversarially tested in this round.
- Serious-effort counts are the counting agents' judgement of what is 'serious'. The underlying paper/tool names and URLs are recorded so the count can be re-derived by a reader who disagrees.


---

# Round 2

### Round 2: adversarial review of a round-1 survivor that was never attacked — Headerless load-layout recovery: base address, ISA/endianness and code/data segmentation for raw firmware blobs (corpus: Assemblage ELF/PE + Debian; manufacturer shape (c) — delete the ELF/PE header, the header IS the label)

**Recommendation: REJECT.** G2a MARGINAL · G3 FAIL · G1 MARGINAL · G6 PASS

**What the manufacturer knows that the student cannot (G2a)**

The manufacturer reads the e_machine / e_ident / p_vaddr / e_entry / section-table bytes that were physically deleted from the student's copy — a crisp answer, but a degenerate one: unlike VPT's future frames, those bytes are a redundant re-encoding of information still present in the body (absolute pointers, ARM literal pools, the reset vector table, opcode statistics), and a published deterministic algorithm reads it straight back out, so the asymmetry is nominal rather than informational and the ceiling is a solved decoding problem, not a teacher.

**Channel capacity (A1)**

MEASURED, AND IT IS THE JAVAC FINDING AGAIN. I downloaded Debian bookworm packages for 8 architectures (amd64, i386, arm64, armhf, armel, mips64el, ppc64el, s390x — riscv64 404s on bookworm) and parsed 838 real ELFs with pyelftools; separately 3,215 ELFs from the local Ubuntu 24.04 root. Base address = min PT_LOAD p_vaddr. Result on the 838 multi-arch set: 7 distinct values ever observed, 0x0 = 98.45%, H(base) = 0.1518 bits. On the 3,215-file set: 4 distinct values, 0x0 = 98.26%, H(base) = 0.1451 bits. Conditioning on the architecture (which ISAdetect already recovers at ~98%) leaves H(base | ISA) = 0.1076 bits, and 8 of the 11 arch classes have H exactly 0. Steelmanning with non-PIE only (ET_EXEC, n=65) it rises to just H = 1.89 bits over 8 distinct values, mode-predictor 50.8%. Weak corroboration on PE: only 3 PE files were reachable in my corpus, all ImageBase 0x400000 — COULD NOT VERIFY at Assemblage's 890k PE scale, but MSVC ImageBase is a documented linker constant (0x400000 / 0x10000000 / 0x140000000 / 0x180000000), so the same degeneracy is expected. The base-address label — the half round 1 hoped was the uncrowded one — is not a function of the input; it is a linker constant carrying about one tenth of one bit. The ISA half by contrast carries H = 2.91 bits and code/data segmentation is genuinely high-entropy, so the bundle is not uniformly zero-capacity — but the two halves that DO carry bits die on A2 and G4 respectively.

**The dumbest baseline (A2)**

THREE DUMB BASELINES, ALL MEASURED ON THIS BOX. (1) Base address, dumbest possible: "always predict 0x0" → 98.45% exact on 838 multi-arch Debian ELFs. (2) Base address, dumb-but-content-aware, ~35 lines, the classic pointer-histogram idea: unpack every word, histogram by page, slide a window of the image's page-width, take the max. On 23 non-PIE nonzero-base ELFs, with zero tuning: 34.8% exact, 65.2% within one page, 82.6% within 16 KiB, 91.3% within 64 KiB. The published versions of this (literal-pool matching, absolute-address statistics) and binbloom do better. (3) ISA + endianness + word size, nearest-centroid on a 256-bin byte histogram of 8 KiB of .text — no training loop, nothing beyond a per-class mean: 93.91% leave-one-out over 11 classes (majority-class baseline 19.92%), n=723. ISAdetect reports ~98% with a real classifier. (4) Code/data segmentation, dumbest possible ("every loaded byte is code"): 49.87% micro / 55.62% macro byte-accuracy over 837 binaries — this is the ONE sub-task with real residual, and it is exactly LOADSTAR. Worst of all, I measured the scaling curve for the ISA task on held-out n=200: 65.6% @ 8 training examples, 89.6% @ 64, 91.7% @ 128, 91.1% @ 256, 90.3% @ 523. It is FLAT from 128 onward — saturated four orders of magnitude below the 10^6 target. That is the precise opposite of the FDM-1 structure the mission requires.

**G3 — kill attempt**

The right kill here is not a frontier model — the input is raw bytes, plain prompting genuinely is not expressible over it, and that part of G3 is fine. The kill is the other clause of G3: an existing NON-LEARNED tool already does this, in production, and the learned curve is measured flat. binbloom (Quarkslab, 585 stars, verified via GitHub) determines loading address AND endianness of raw headerless firmware by heuristic statistics; rbasefind and Ghidra's base-finder do the same; radare2/rabin2 and ISAdetect cover the ISA half. Behind them sits a decade of dedicated algorithms: literal-pool matching (Digital Investigation 2016, 22 cit), image-base determination for ARM (IEICE Trans. 2016, 19 cit), ICS firmware image base (Int. J. Critical Infrastructure Protection 2017, 31 cit), MIPS absolute-address statistics + string reference matching (Computers & Security 2020), function-address matching (WCMC 2021), image-base range (LNNS 2023). My own untuned 35-line reimplementation of the idea hit 91.3% within 64 KiB. A learned model trained on 10^6 units would be competing against a deterministic decoder for a label that carries 0.11 bits conditional on the arch.

**G3 — does it survive?**

Nothing survives intact. The bundle has three sub-tasks and each dies to a different gate, so they do not recombine into a survivor: base address dies on A1 (0.15 bits of label entropy, measured) and on six dedicated published algorithms; ISA/endianness dies on A2 (93.9% from a nearest-centroid with no training loop, curve flat past n=128) and on ISAdetect/ELISA/Clemens; code/data segmentation is the only high-entropy residual and it is precisely "You Can't Judge a Binary by Its Header: Data-Code Separation for Non-Standard ARM Binaries Using Pseudo Labels" (IEEE S&P 2025) — the identical header-deletion pseudo-label trick, already published at a top-4 security venue. The only conceivable narrowing — bare-metal MCU firmware, where the base genuinely does vary (0x08000000 STM32, 0xBFC00000 MIPS reset, 0x9D000000 PIC32) — fails the manufacturer gate outright: real bare-metal images ship with NO header, so there is nothing to delete and no label to read. You would have to synthesise the corpus yourself with randomised linker scripts, at which point you are teaching a model your own linker-script prior, and the pointer-histogram heuristic recovers it anyway.

**G1 — licence reality**

Mixed, and MARGINAL for the Phase-3 shipping requirement. Debian is clean: I actually fetched Packages.gz and ~480 .deb files across 8 architectures from http://deb.debian.org/debian/dists/bookworm/main/binary-{amd64,i386,arm64,armhf,armel,mips64el,ppc64el,s390x}/ — all HTTP 200, no robots.txt served at all (404), and Debian main is DFSG-free and explicitly redistributable. Assemblage is the problem: https://huggingface.co/api/datasets/changliu8541/Assemblage_LinuxELF and .../Assemblage_PE both carry cardData {"license": "other"}, tag license:other, ungated, ~375 GB, arxiv:2405.03991. The dataset card states the Assemblage CODE is MIT but "individual binaries retain their original repository licenses" — i.e. there is no blanket redistribution grant covering the 428,884 ELF / 890,000 PE artifacts drawn from 30,856 GitHub repos. Shipping an open artifact derived from that would require a per-repo licence audit across 30,856 repos, a large fraction of which on GitHub carry no licence at all (= all rights reserved). Not fatal on its own — you could build the whole study on Debian main — but it removes the "428k + 890k units" abundance claim as a redistributable corpus, and Debian alone at 10^6 units is a stretch.

**G4 — re-check**

FAIL, and worse than round 1 assessed — round 1 said MARGINAL 6/10 and hoped the base-address half was open. It is not. I ran 12+ query formulations across 4 domain-appropriate indices (arXiv API, dblp, Crossref, EuropePMC, HuggingFace datasets API, GitHub). BASE-ADDRESS HALF, six dedicated papers: "Determining image base of firmware for ARM devices by matching literal pools" (Digital Investigation 2016, 22 cit); "Determining Image Base of Firmware Files for ARM Devices" (IEICE Trans. Inf. Syst. 2016, 19 cit); "A methodology for determining the image base of ARM-based industrial control system firmware" (Int. J. Critical Infrastructure Protection 2017, 31 cit); "Determining the base address of MIPS firmware based on absolute address statistics and string reference matching" (Computers & Security 2020, dblp-confirmed, authors Zhu/Zhang/Jiang/Chang); "Determining the Image Base of ARM Firmware by Matching Function Addresses" (Wireless Comm. & Mobile Computing 2021); "Determining the Range of Image Base of ARM Firmware" (LNNS 2023). Plus production tooling: binbloom (github.com/quarkslab/binbloom, 585 stars, base address + endianness on raw firmware) and rbasefind. ISA/SEGMENTATION HALF, confirming round 1: ISAdetect (CODASPY 2020, dblp-confirmed); arXiv 1908.05459 "Towards usable automated detection of CPU architecture and endianness for arbitrary binary files"; arXiv 2204.06624 "A Natural Language Processing Approach for Instruction Set Architecture Identification"; arXiv 2410.21558 (Andreassen & Morrison 2024) "Discovery of Endianness and Instruction Size Characteristics in Binary Programs from Unknown Instruction Sets"; and dblp-confirmed IEEE S&P 2025 "You Can't Judge a Binary by Its Header: Data-Code Separation for Non-Standard ARM Binaries Using Pseudo Labels" — the same header-as-pseudo-label mechanism this proposal claims as its novelty. Total ~13 serious efforts against a gate of "< ~3". HuggingFace has no dataset for this task at any scale (searches for firmware+binary, binary+base+address, raw+binary+architecture all returned zero relevant hits), which is the one thing genuinely missing — but an absent dataset for a task that 13 papers and 2 production tools already solve heuristically is not white space, it is a task nobody needs a dataset for.

**G6 — feasibility**

G6 is the one gate this passes cleanly, and it does not matter. Measured over 725 real ELFs: median 159.3 KiB, mean 749.1 KiB, p90 1393 KiB. Full binaries at 10^6 units = ~0.16 TB, well over the ~30 GB disk. But the task only needs a window: truncating to a 4 KiB byte-window per unit gives 4.1 GB at 10^6, and 16 KiB gives 16 GB — both fit 30 GB disk with the smaller rungs. Models: a 256-bin byte histogram plus n-gram features feeds a linear/GBM classifier that trains on 10^6 rows in minutes on 4 cores; a small 1-D byte-CNN (~1-5 M params, seq len 4096) fits far inside 15 GB and would run maybe 2-6 CPU-hours per epoch at 10^6 on 4 cores, so four rungs at 1k/10k/100k/1M is a few days at most. Feasible. But feasibility is irrelevant when the measured curve is already flat at n=128 and the base-address label carries 0.11 bits conditional on the arch — you would be spending days of CPU to draw a horizontal line.

**Strongest case against**

The proposal bundles three sub-tasks and every one of them dies, each to a different gate, so there is no recombination and no rescue. (1) The base-address half — the ONLY reason this candidate survived round 1 — has a label that is a linker constant: measured H(base) = 0.152 bits over 838 multi-arch Debian ELFs, H(base | ISA) = 0.108 bits, and "always predict 0x0" scores 98.45% exact. This is round 1's javac kill in a new costume. (2) The ISA/endianness half is solved to 93.91% by a nearest-centroid on a byte histogram with no training loop, and the measured scaling curve is FLAT from 128 examples onward (91.7% @ 128 → 90.3% @ 523) — four orders of magnitude below the 10^6 target, which inverts the entire FDM-1 thesis. (3) The code/data half is the only one with real residual (dumb baseline 50-56%) and it is verbatim IEEE S&P 2025 LOADSTAR, the same header-deletion pseudo-label trick, published at a top-tier venue. Meanwhile the "asymmetry" is nominal: the deleted header is a redundant copy of information still sitting in the body, which binbloom (585 stars, in production) and six published papers already read back out deterministically — so this is not distillation from a teacher, it is a learned approximation of a solved decoder, which is strictly the worst position to be in. And the buyers named (ONEKEY, NetRise, Binary Ninja) already ship binbloom-class heuristics, so the economic tail is being sold a slower version of what they have. The founder's stated failure mode was building a specialised technique that a general approach matched; here a 10-line non-learned rule matches it, today, at n=128.

### Round 2: adversarial review of a round-1 survivor that was never attacked — ProcedureNet — recovering coded flight procedures (SID/STAR/approach) and ARINC 424 leg decompositions from ADS-B trajectories, labelled by geometric matching against the FAA CIFP

**Recommendation: REJECT.** G2a FAIL · G3 FAIL · G1 MARGINAL · G6 PASS

**What the manufacturer knows that the student cannot (G2a)**

Almost nothing: the manufacturer is a nearest-polyline match against the FAA CIFP, a 9 MB public file that is equally available to the student and runs in milliseconds at deployment — unlike VPT's IDM, which structurally cannot run at inference — so the only real asymmetry is "manufacturer sees the whole flight, student sees a prefix," and I measured that asymmetry to be worth 13 percentage points (a zero-learning nearest-route rule applied to just the first 40% of the terminal segment already reproduces 87.0% of the full-trajectory label, 395/454 real arrivals).

**Channel capacity (A1)**

Varies, but carries very few bits, and the residual bits are NOT a function of the input. Measured from CIFP 2608 (parsed 396,460 records -> 93,253 waypoints, 52,404 procedure transitions, 3,070 airports): label cardinality is 3-20 STARs per airport (KJFK 3, KBOS 3, KSEA 4, KMIA 6, KATL 8, KORD 11, KDFW 20, KLAX 20) — roughly 3.3 bits before conditioning. And the classes are geometrically enormous distances apart: pairwise separation between DISTINCT STAR transitions at the same airport has median 44.7-136.4 nm across 14 busy airports, with only 0.0-9.6% of pairs closer than 5 nm and 0.0-3.6% closer than 2 nm (KATL 3,152 pairs: median 77.3 nm, 2.1% within 5 nm; KDEN 5,974 pairs: median 76.1 nm, 0.8% within 5 nm). So the geometry-to-label map is near-deterministic. Where it is NOT — on 27.1% of the cases where the dumb rule disagreed, the two rival published procedures were within 1.0 nm of each other (37.3% within 2.0 nm) — the two candidates occupy essentially the same line in space, so which one ATC actually cleared lives in the flight plan and the clearance, not in lat/lon/alt. That is A1 zero-capacity on precisely the residual the model would have to earn. Worse, 61.7% of real arrivals (731/1185) had no published procedure within 4 nm at all — vectored or off-procedure — and for those the manufacturer produces nothing and nothing can verify a pseudo-label. That is the round-1 crystallography kill repeated: the commercially interesting cases are exactly the ones with no obtainable label.

**The dumbest baseline (A2)**

Measured on real data, on this box. Corpus: adsb.lol globe_history 2023-09-01 (ODbL), 1.6 GB streamed and untarred -> 44,742 aircraft traces -> 3,720 real US terminal arrivals at 14 major airports; 1,185 scored against CIFP-derived STAR routes. Dumb baseline = ~40 lines of numpy, no learning: resample each published STAR route (enroute transition + common + runway transition) to 0.5 nm, take mean over route points of min great-circle distance to the trajectory, pick the argmin. (1) As the manufacturer over full trajectories it labels 38.3% of arrivals at fit < 4 nm. (2) As the STUDENT's competitor — the same rule fed only the first 40% of the terminal segment — it recovers the manufacturer's full-trajectory label 395/454 = 87.0% of the time. Median best-vs-second-best margin is 6.77 nm where it agrees and 3.56 nm where it disagrees, and 37.3% of disagreements have rivals within 2 nm. So a non-learned rule with no training data takes 87% of the label, and roughly a third of the remaining 13% is a degenerate label rather than a hard problem. This is the COBOL record-layout pattern from round 1 (50-line rule, F1 0.768) reproduced almost exactly, at a higher number. The curve would be drawn over a ~8-point residual, much of it label noise. Scripts: /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-raise-v1/5d675fa9-5fc4-501c-b3b3-a76510397d00/scratchpad/parse_cifp.py, geom.py, extract.py, match2.py, match3.py; output in match3.out.

**G3 — kill attempt**

Two kill attempts. (a) Frontier general model with plain prompting: this one FAILS to kill. The input is a numeric field (lat/lon/alt/gs/track/vs time series), outside web-text pretraining distribution; plain prompting is not naturally expressible and an LLM has no reason to hold 52,404 CIFP transitions in weights. The G3 heuristic protects this candidate on that axis. (b) Existing non-learned tool: this one KILLS it. xoolive/traffic (PyPI `traffic` 2.13, mature, maintained, free) ships Flight.aligned_on_navpoint(), Flight.aligned_on_ils(), Flight.aligned_on() with method in {'default','beacon','ils','runway'}, LandingAlignedOnILS, RunwayAlignment, and a point_merge algorithm built on aligned_on_navpoint (verified via GitHub code search in src/traffic/core/flight.py and src/traffic/algorithms/navigation/point_merge.py). The entire shape-(c) manufacturer is an off-the-shelf library call over a public 9 MB database. You would be training a neural network to imitate a free deterministic function that anyone can also call at inference time. (c) Scale monotonicity independently: a ~3.3-bit airport-conditioned label over a near-deterministic geometric map saturates fast; the extra 900k examples buy you the vectored tail, whose labels are unverifiable noise. A bigger model does not eat this, but a 40-line function does — which is the same death.

**G3 — does it survive?**

Only this much survives: the input modality is genuinely out of LLM distribution, and the CPU/data engineering is real and cheap. Nothing about the LEARNING thesis survives. The failure mode here is not the founder's old one (bigger model with plain prompting matches it) — it is the adjacent and equally fatal one: a free deterministic tool with no learning matches it, and gets better, not worse, as the manufacturer improves. Note the perverse structure: any improvement to the geometric matcher raises the fraction the free rule labels and therefore SHRINKS the residual the model is supposed to own. The thesis gets worse as the manufacturer gets better, which is the same shape of un-fundability.

**G1 — licence reality**

As proposed (OpenSky Network) this is a hard FAIL, verified verbatim from https://opensky-network.org/about/terms-of-use (fetched 2026-08-25; the page 403s to plain WebFetch, retrieved with a browser User-Agent). §1 LICENSE: the grant is "solely for the purpose of non-profit research and non-profit education"; "Any use by a for-profit or commercial entity requires written permission and a license granted by the OpenSky Network. This includes any and all government and military contractors." The banner at the top of the page repeats it. §3(iii): "You will not distribute, disclose, transfer or otherwise make available the data set(s) to any person other than those employed by your institute" — that alone kills the Phase 3 open artifact. §3(iv): all restrictions propagate to any derived disclosure. §4(ii): "You agree to expunge any and all copies of the received data set(s) upon completion or termination of stated research." §6(i), for commercial licensees: any "invention, discovery, improvement, computer or other program... developed for commercial use by You... shall be the joint property of OpenSky Network and You, and that shared portion is hereby assigned to OpenSky Network" — a default IP-assignment clause that is a company-killer on its own.
